=== BUGGY CODE ===
class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True
        
        def isForbidden(s):
            t = trie
            counter = 0
            for c in s:
                if c not in t:
                    //return False
                t = t[c]
                counter += 1
                if "end" in t:
                    return counter
            return False
        
        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(word[i:j])
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res

Observe the following faulty python code. It contains one or more bugs.

```python
class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True
        
        def isForbidden(s):
            t = trie
            counter = 0
            for c in s:
                if c not in t:
                    //return False
                t = t[c]
                counter += 1
                if "end" in t:
                    return counter
            return False
        
        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(word[i:j])
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```

Reason in a repair-and-recheck way.

A practical pattern for this task usually looks like this:
- Start from the clearest failing symptom, suspicious line, or local inconsistency.
- Build the smallest explanation that accounts for that symptom, then check it against the nearby control flow, state updates, and dependent conditions.
- Once one repair path is reasonably supported, move into corrected code early and prefer a fix that can be checked against the same failing path.
- After the edit, rerun that path or the most relevant edge case.
- Then do one small coupled-region scan: a directly connected guard, index update, return path, or call site that could still break the same fix.
- If the recheck is still off, refine the same local region before opening a distant new theory.
- Once the failing path and its directly coupled region look consistent, finalize the code.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- staying in diagnosis mode for too long without testing the next plausible edit;
- moving from a local repair into a fresh branch of speculative causes when the current path has not been rechecked yet;
- turning a focused fix into a broad rewrite unless the surrounding code clearly forces it.

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True
        
        def isForbidden(s):
            t = trie
            counter = 0
            for c in s:
                if c not in t:
                    //return False
                t = t[c]
                counter += 1
                if "end" in t:
                    return counter
            return False
        
        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(word[i:j])
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res

Base: i = n-1, j = n. word[n-1:n] = last char. If forbidden prefix found, j = n-1 + L -1. Since L=1 if last char forbidden? Actually if last char is forbidden single char, j=n-1. If last char not forbidden, j=n. res = j-(n-1) = 1 if valid, 0 if forbidden. Correct.

Inductive step: Assume before processing i, j is the maximum exclusive end such that word[i+1:j] is valid (contains no forbidden substring). Actually after processing i+1, j is maximum for i+1. Then for i, consider word[i:j]. If valid, then j is maximum for i? Need prove. Suppose there exists r > j such that word[i:r] valid. Then word[i+1:r] is a substring of word[i:r], so valid. But j was maximum for i+1, so r <= j, contradiction. Thus j max. If word[i:j] invalid, since word[i+1:j] valid, any forbidden substring in word[i:j] must start at i. Let L be the length of the shortest forbidden prefix of word[i:j]. Then word[i:i+L] forbidden, and no shorter prefix forbidden. For any r > i+L-1, word[i:r] contains that forbidden prefix, so invalid. For r = i+L-1, word[i:r] length L-1, and because no forbidden substring starts at i of length <= L-1 and word[i+1:r] is a prefix of valid word[i+1:j]? Wait r = i+L-1 <= j? Since L <= j-i, r <= j-1? Actually if L = j-i, r = j-1. word[i+1:r] is a prefix of word[i+1:j], so valid. Thus word[i:r] valid. So maximum exclusive end is i+L-1. Code sets j to that. Correct.

Now, need decide final code. We can either minimally fix:
```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        trie = {}
        for f in forbidden:
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True
        
        def isForbidden(s):
            t = trie
            counter = 0
            for c in s:
                if c not in t:
                    return False
                t = t[c]
                counter += 1
                if "end" in t:
                    return counter
            return False
        
        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            truc = isForbidden(word[i:j])
            if truc:
                j = i + truc - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```
This fixes syntax and missing return. But O(n^2) due to slicing word[i:j] each time (also creates substring O(n)). Actually slicing word[i:j] creates a copy O(length), so even worse. We can avoid slicing by passing word and i, j to isForbidden and scanning indices. But if we only fix, hidden tests may TLE. The prompt says "faulty python code" and "repair-and-recheck way" but final code should be correct. We should improve to efficient.

Let's examine LeetCode 2781 constraints: `1 <= word.length <= 10^5`, `forbidden.length <= 10^5`, `1 <= forbidden[i].length <= 10`. So O(n^2) definitely TLE. The original code with slicing is O(n^2) and memory. The intended solution uses sliding window with forbidden set and max length 10. But the provided code uses trie and reverse iteration. We can optimize to O(n * max_len) by scanning at most max_len chars and avoiding slicing.

Let's design fixed efficient code preserving approach.

Option 1: Keep reverse iteration and trie, but modify isForbidden to accept `word`, `start`, `end`, and `max_len`, and scan from start up to min(end, start+max_len). Then no slicing. Complexity O(n * max_len). Let's write:

```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        if not forbidden:
            return len(word)

        trie = {}
        max_len = 0
        for f in forbidden:
            max_len = max(max_len, len(f))
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def forbidden_prefix_length(start: int, end: int) -> int:
            t = trie
            limit = min(end, start + max_len)
            for pos in range(start, limit):
                c = word[pos]
                if c not in t:
                    return 0
                t = t[c]
                if "end" in t:
                    return pos - start + 1
            return 0

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            length = forbidden_prefix_length(i, j)
            if length:
                j = i + length - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```

Need check: `forbidden_prefix_length` returns 0 if no forbidden prefix. If `t` becomes a node and "end" in t, return length. If c not in t, return 0. If we reach limit without finding, return 0. Good.

But wait: We set `max_len = max(len(f) for f in forbidden)`. If forbidden list non-empty. Good.

Is it safe to limit scan to `start + max_len`? Suppose a forbidden word length > max_len? impossible. If a forbidden prefix length L <= max_len, scanning up to max_len will find it. Good.

Now, is the reverse iteration with max_len still correct? Yes.

But let's think about `j` update. Suppose `forbidden_prefix_length` returns length L of shortest forbidden prefix starting at i in word[i:j]. We set j = i + L - 1. Then window length L-1. But what if L-1 > max_len? impossible because L <= max_len. Good.

Potential issue: If `j` is less than `i + max_len`, limit = j. Good.

Potential issue: If `j` is large and a forbidden prefix starts at i but has length > j-i? impossible because limit = j. Good.

Potential issue: If `forbidden` contains duplicate strings, trie end set. max_len okay.

Potential issue: If `forbidden` contains empty string? max_len 0, but constraints no. If empty string, every substring invalid? Not handle. ignore.

Potential issue: The code uses `List` type; include import.

Standard solution:
```
left = 0
ans = 0
for right in range(n):
    for end in range(right, max(right-10, -1), -1):
        if word[end:right+1] in forbidden:
            left = end + 1
            break
    ans = max(ans, right-left+1)
```
This maintains window [left, right] valid. The reverse algorithm maintains [i, j) valid. Let's map. At each i, j is exclusive right boundary. The length is j-i. It effectively finds the earliest forbidden substring ending at or after i? Actually reverse.

Now, consider if forbidden = ["abc"], word = "ababc", at i=2, limit=min(5,5)=5, scans "abc" and returns 3. Good.

Now, let's think about the original code's `isForbidden` returning `counter` when "end" in t. If a forbidden word is found, it returns immediately. But what if there is a shorter forbidden word that is a suffix of the current prefix? Since we scan from left, any forbidden word ending at current position is a suffix of the scanned prefix. The trie check `"end" in t` detects if the entire scanned prefix is forbidden. It does not detect if a suffix is forbidden unless that suffix is also a prefix of the scanned prefix? Wait, this is important! Let's examine.

The trie is built from forbidden words. To check if any forbidden word is a prefix of `s`, we traverse from root following characters of `s`. At each step, if the node has "end", then the prefix of `s` from start to current position is a forbidden word. This detects forbidden words that are prefixes of `s`. It does NOT detect forbidden words that occur as a suffix of the scanned prefix but not starting at the beginning. For example, forbidden = ["bc"], s = "abc". The trie root has 'b'. Scanning s: pos0 'a' not in root -> return False. But "bc" occurs in s starting at pos1. However, in our algorithm, we call `forbidden_prefix_length` for each left index i. For i=1, s = word[1:j] = "bc", so it will detect. For i=0, we only need to detect forbidden substrings starting at 0. So prefix detection is sufficient. Good.

But wait, when we call `forbidden_prefix_length(i, j)`, we are checking forbidden substrings starting at i. But what about forbidden substrings that start at i but are not prefixes of word[i:j] because they extend beyond j? We only care within current window. If a forbidden substring starting at i extends beyond j, then it's not contained in word[i:j], so doesn't invalidate. But when j is maximum for i+1, could there be a forbidden substring starting at i that extends beyond j but not within j? If it extends beyond j, then any window [i:r] with r <= j doesn't contain it, so it doesn't affect validity. For r > j, previous window [i+1:r] would be invalid? Let's think. Suppose forbidden substring starts at i and extends beyond j. For r > j, word[i:r] contains it, so invalid. But j is maximum for i+1, so r > j invalid for i+1. So no need. Thus checking only within j is enough.

Now, another subtlety: The trie traversal in `forbidden_prefix_length` stops when `c not in t`. But what if a forbidden word is a prefix of the current path, and then the next char is not in t? We would have returned at the end of that forbidden word before moving to next char. So okay. What if no forbidden word ends at current node, but the current node has no child for next char; then no forbidden word can start at i with longer length, so return 0. Good.

Now, let's consider if the trie's "end" key could be confused with a child character if a forbidden word contains the string "end". But word characters are lowercase letters, so "end" is not a single character. However, in Python, a key can be a multi-character string, but we only insert single characters as keys. The special key "end" is a string of length 3. When checking `if c not in t`, c is a single character, so it won't match "end". When checking `if "end" in t`, it checks the special key. Good.

Potential issue: If a forbidden word is empty string, then `t["end"] = True` at root. Then `forbidden_prefix_length` would need to check root before loop. Not in constraints.

```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        if not forbidden:
            return len(word)

        trie = {}
        max_len = 0
        for f in forbidden:
            max_len = max(max_len, len(f))
            t = trie
            for c in f:
                if c not in t:
                    t[c] = {}
                t = t[c]
            t["end"] = True

        def forbidden_prefix_length(start: int, end: int) -> int:
            t = trie
            limit = min(end, start + max_len)
            for pos in range(start, limit):
                c = word[pos]
                if c not in t:
                    return 0
                t = t[c]
                if "end" in t:
                    return pos - start + 1
            return 0

        res = 0
        j = len(word)
        for i in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            length = forbidden_prefix_length(i, j)
            if length:
                j = i + length - 1
            res = max(res, j - i)
        return res
```

Now, let's decide final code style. We can write:

```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        if not forbidden:
            return len(word)

        trie = {}
        max_len = 0
        for f in forbidden:
            max_len = max(max_len, len(f))
            node = trie
            for ch in f:
                if ch not in node:
                    node[ch] = {}
                node = node[ch]
            node["end"] = True

        def first_forbidden_len(start: int, end: int) -> int:
            node = trie
            limit = min(end, start + max_len)
            for pos in range(start, limit):
                ch = word[pos]
                if ch not in node:
                    return 0
                node = node[ch]
                if "end" in node:
                    return pos - start + 1
            return 0

        ans = 0
        right = len(word)
        for left in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            length = first_forbidden_len(left, right)
            if length:
                right = left + length - 1
            ans = max(ans, right - left)
        return ans
```

This is clean. Use `right` instead of `j` for clarity.

left=0: limit=min(2,2)=2, pos0 'a', node; pos1 'a', node; end -> return2, right=0+2-1=1, ans=max(1,1)=1. OK.

=== RAW ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        if not forbidden:
            return len(word)

        trie = {}
        max_len = 0
        for f in forbidden:
            max_len = max(max_len, len(f))
            node = trie
            for ch in f:
                if ch not in node:
                    node[ch] = {}
                node = node[ch]
            node["end"] = True

        def forbidden_prefix_length(start: int, end: int) -> int:
            node = trie
            limit = min(end, start + max_len)
            for pos in range(start, limit):
                ch = word[pos]
                if ch not in node:
                    return 0
                node = node[ch]
                if "end" in node:
                    return pos - start + 1
            return 0

        ans = 0
        right = len(word)

        for left in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            length = forbidden_prefix_length(left, right)
            if length:
                right = left + length - 1
            ans = max(ans, right - left)

        return ans
```

=== FIXED CODE ===
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def longestValidSubstring(self, word: str, forbidden: List[str]) -> int:
        if not forbidden:
            return len(word)

        trie = {}
        max_len = 0
        for f in forbidden:
            max_len = max(max_len, len(f))
            node = trie
            for ch in f:
                if ch not in node:
                    node[ch] = {}
                node = node[ch]
            node["end"] = True

        def forbidden_prefix_length(start: int, end: int) -> int:
            node = trie
            limit = min(end, start + max_len)
            for pos in range(start, limit):
                ch = word[pos]
                if ch not in node:
                    return 0
                node = node[ch]
                if "end" in node:
                    return pos - start + 1
            return 0

        ans = 0
        right = len(word)

        for left in range(len(word) - 1, -1, -1):
            length = forbidden_prefix_length(left, right)
            if length:
                right = left + length - 1
            ans = max(ans, right - left)

        return ans
